Probabilistic Guarded KAT Modulo Bisimilarity: Completeness and Complexity
Wojciech Różowski, Tobias Kappé, Dexter Kozen, Todd Schmid, Alexandra Silva

Abstract
We introduce Probabilistic Guarded Kleene Algebra with Tests ( 𝖯𝗋𝗈𝖻𝖦𝖪𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{ProbGKAT} ), an extension of 𝖦𝖪𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{GKAT} that allows reasoning about uninterpreted imperative programs with probabilistic branching. We give its operational semantics in terms of special class of probabilistic automata. We give a sound and complete Salomaa-style axiomatisation of bisimilarity of 𝖯𝗋𝗈𝖻𝖦𝖪𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{ProbGKAT} expressions. Finally, we show that bisimilarity of 𝖯𝗋𝗈𝖻𝖦𝖪𝖠𝖳 \mathsf{ProbGKAT} expressions can be decided in O ⁡ ( n 3 ​ log ⁡ n ) O(n^{3}\log n) time via a generic partition refinement algorithm.

1
Introduction

Randomisation is an important feature in the design of efficient algorithms, cryptographic protocols, and stochastic simulation
[
10
]
.
For a simple example of randomisation, imagine simulating a three-sided die
[
70
]
.
There are at least two ways to do this:

•

A reference implementation could use a fair coin and a biased coin with probability
\frac{1}{3}
of landing on
heads
:
Toss the biased coin first.
If it lands on
heads
, return
, and otherwise toss the fair coin and return
if it lands on
heads
or
otherwise.

•

Another way to do this is with two consecutive tosses of a fair coin: if the outcome is
heads-heads
, then return
; if it is
heads-tails
, return
; if it is
tails-heads
, return
; and if it is
tails-tails
, repeat the process
[
40
]
.

These programs can be written using a function
\texttt{flip}(p)
that returns
true
(
heads
) with probability
{{p}}
, and
false
(
tails
) with probability
{{1-p}}
, see
Fig.

1
.
If we can prove that those programs are equivalent, then we can be certain they implement the same distribution.

while

true

do

if

\mathsf{flip(0.5)}

then

if

\mathsf{flip(0.5)}

then

return

else

return

else

if

\mathsf{flip(0.5)}

then

return

else

skip

if

\mathsf{flip(1/3)}

then

return

else

if

\mathsf{flip(0.5)}

then

return

else

return

Figure 1:
On the left, the Knuth-Yao process to simulate a three-sided die with two throws of a fair coin. On the right, direct implementation of the three-sided die (note that the probability of the first else-branch is
\frac{2}{3}
, hence both
and
are returned with probability
\frac{1}{3}
).

In this paper, we introduce Probabilistic
\mathsf{GKAT}
(
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
), a language based on Guarded Kleene Algebra with Tests (
\mathsf{GKAT}
)
[
45
,
64
,
59
]
augmented with extra constructs for reasoning about such randomised programs.
The laws of
\mathsf{GKAT}
allow reasoning about the equivalence of uninterpreted programs with deterministic control flow in the form of Boolean branching (
if-then-else
) and looping (
while-do
) constructs.

\mathsf{GKAT}
comes equipped with an automata-theoretic operational semantics, a
nearly linear
decision procedure, and complete axiomatic systems for reasoning about trace equivalence
[
64
]
and bisimilarity
[
59
]
of expressions, both inspired by Salomaa’s axiomatisation of Kleene Algebra
[
58
]
.

\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
extends
\mathsf{GKAT}
with three new syntactic constructs:
(1) a probabilistic choice operator, representing branching based on a (possibly biased) coin flip;
(2) a probabilistic loop operator, representing a generalised Bernoulli process; and
(3) return values, which allow a limited form of non-local control flow akin to
return
statements in imperative programming.

The main focus of this paper is the problem of axiomatising bisimilarity of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions.
We build on an inference system for reasoning about bisimilarity of
\mathsf{GKAT}
expressions
[
59
]
, which includes a generalisation of Salomaa’s axiomatisation of the Kleene star
[
58
]
called the Uniqueness of Solutions axiom (
\mathsf{UA}
), also known in the process algebra community as Recursive Specification Principle (
\mathsf{RSP}
)
[
11
]
.
In the presence of both Boolean guarded and probabilistic branching, axiomatisation becomes significantly more involved.
Besides adding intuitive rules governing the behaviour of probabilistic choice and loops, we add axioms capturing the interaction of both kinds of branching when combined with looping constructs.
Moreover, in the case of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
, showing the soundness of
\mathsf{UA}
becomes highly nontrivial.
We do so by exploiting the topological structure of the operational model, namely the
behavioural pseudometric
associated with bisimilarity.
Despite the jump in difficulty, our completeness proof follows a similar strategy as the one for
\mathsf{GKAT}
modulo bisimilarity
[
59
]
.

Our main contributions are as follows.

•

We provide an operational semantics of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
programs, which relies on a type of automata that have both Boolean guarded and probabilistic transitions (
Section

3
).

•

We concretely characterise bisimulations for our automata using an adaption of the flow network characterisation of bisimilarity of Markov chains
[
36
,
9
]
(
Section

4
).

•

We give a sound and complete Salomaa-style axiomatisation of bisimulation equivalence of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions (
Sections

5
and
6
).

•

We show that, when the number of tests is fixed, bisimilarity of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions can be efficiently decided in
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\log n)
time, where
n
is the total size of programs under comparison, by using
coalgebraic partition refinement

[
20
,
73
]
(
Section

7
).

In
Section

2
we define the syntax of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
.
We survey related work in
Section

8
; conclusions and further work appear in
Section

9
.
Proofs appear in the appendix.

2
Syntax

\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
has a two-sorted syntax consisting of a set of
expressions

\mathtt{Exp}
that contains a set
\mathtt{BExp}
of
Boolean assertions
or
tests
.
For a fixed finite set
\mathsf{T}
of
primitive tests
, the syntax for tests is denoted
\mathtt{BExp}
and generated by the grammar

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\in\mathtt{BExp}::={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}\mid t\in\mathsf{T}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\mid\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}

Here,
0
and
1
respectively denote false and true,
\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\cdot}}}
denotes negation,
+
is disjunction, and juxtaposition is conjunction.
Let
\equiv_{\mathsf{BA}}
denote Boolean equivalence in
\mathtt{BExp}
. Entailment is a preorder on
\mathtt{BExp}
given by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\iff{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\equiv_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}
. The quotient of
\mathtt{BExp}
by
\equiv_{\mathsf{BA}}
is the free Boolean algebra on the set of generators
\mathsf{T}
, in which entailment—
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}]_{\equiv_{\mathsf{BA}}}\leq[{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}]_{\equiv_{\mathsf{BA}}}\iff{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}
—is a partial order, with bottom and top elements being the equivalence classes of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
respectively. The minimal non-zero elements of that partial order are called
atoms
, and we will use
\mathsf{At}
to denote the set of atoms.
For fixed sets
\mathsf{Act}
of
atomic actions
and
\mathsf{Out}
of
return values
, the set
\mathtt{Exp}
of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions is defined by the grammar in
Figure

2
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}::=

\displaystyle\hskip 8.30002pt\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}

\displaystyle\mathsf{do}~\mathsf{p}

\displaystyle\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}

\displaystyle\mathsf{assert}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{e}+_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}~\mathsf{then}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{else}~\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}

\displaystyle\mathsf{while}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}~\mathsf{do}~\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle\mid{{v}}\in\mathsf{Out}

\displaystyle\mathsf{return}~{{v}}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}~\mathsf{flip(}{{r}}\mathsf{)}~\mathsf{then}~\mathsf{e}~\mathsf{else}~\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}

\displaystyle\mathsf{while}~\mathsf{flip(}{{r}}\mathsf{)}~\mathsf{do}~\mathsf{e}

Figure 2:
Syntax of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
.

The syntax of
\mathsf{GKAT}
is captured by the first five cases in
Fig.

2
, and so is a proper fragment of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
.
There are three new constructs:
return values
,
probabilistic choices
, and
probabilistic loops
.
Return values behave like return statements in imperative programs, introducing a form of non-local control flow.
The probabilistic choice
\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f}
flips a biased coin with real bias
{{r}}\in[0,1]
and depending on the outcome runs
\mathsf{e}
with probability
{{r}}
and
\mathsf{f}
with probability
{{1-r}}
.
The probabilistic loop
\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}
also begins with a biased coin flip, and depending on the outcome it either executes
\mathsf{e}
and starts again (probability
r
) or terminates (probability
1-r
).
A probabilistic loop can be regarded as a generalised Bernoulli processes.

Example 1
.

Recall the two programs from the introduction (
Fig.

1
): one directly implementing a 3-sided die and the other simulating a 3-sided die with a fair coin.
We can express these programs using three output values,
,
, and
, to model the possible outcomes of the three-sided die.
The first program is the infinite while loop
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}})}
, where the loop body is given by
\mathsf{g}=(\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice})\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}})
.
In
\mathsf{f}
,
represents
heads-heads
,
is
heads-tails
,
is
tails-heads
, and
tails-tails
prompts a rethrow.
The second program encodes the
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expression
\mathsf{e}=\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{3}}}}(\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice})
.

3
Operational semantics

In this section, we formally introduce
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata, the operational models of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions.
We associate a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton with each expression via a small-step semantics inspired by Brzozowski derivatives
[
14
]
.
As we will see, the biggest hurdle is the semantics of the probabilistic loop.
Before we provide our small-step semantics, we introduce the notation and operations on probability distributions that we will need.

Preliminary definitions.

A function
\nu:X\to[0,1]
is called a
(probability) distribution on
X
if it satisfies
\sum_{x\in X}\nu(x)=1
.
In case
\sum_{x\in X}\nu(x)\leq 1
we call
\nu
a
subprobability distribution
, or
subdistribution
.
Every (sub)distribution
\nu
in this paper is
finitely supported
, which means that the set
\mathtt{supp}(\nu)=\{x\in X\mid\nu(x)>0\}
is finite.
Given
A\subseteq X
, we define
\nu[A]=\sum_{x\in A}\nu(x)
.
This sum is well-defined because only finitely many summands have non-zero probability.

We use
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(X)
to denote the set of finitely supported probability distributions on the set
X
.
A function
f:X\to Y
can be
lifted
to a map
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(f):\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(Y)
between distributions by setting
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(f)(\nu)=\nu[f^{-1}(y)]
.
Given
x\in X
, its
Dirac delta
is the distribution
\delta_{x}
; here
\delta_{x}(y)
is equal to
1
when
x=y
, and
0
otherwise.
Given
f:X\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(Y)
, there is a unique map
\bar{f}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(Y)
such that
f=\bar{f}\circ\delta
, called the
convex extension of
f
, and explicitly given by
\bar{f}(\nu)(y)=\sum_{x\in X}\nu(x)f(x)(y)
.

When
\nu,\mu:X\to[0,1]
are probability distributions and
r\in[0,1]
, we write
r\nu+(1-r)\mu
for the
convex combination
of
\nu
and
\mu
, which is the probability distribution given by
(r\nu+(1-r)\mu)(x)=r\nu(x)+(1-r)\mu(x)
; this operation preserves finite support.

Operational model.

Operationally,
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions denote states in a transition system called a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
.
Below, we write
\mathsf{2}=\{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

for a two element set of symbols denoting rejection and acceptance respectively.

Definition 2
.

A
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
is a pair
(X,\beta)
consisting of set of
states

X
and a
transition function

\beta:X\times\mathsf{At}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times X)
.

A state in a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton associates each Boolean atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
(capturing the global state of the Boolean variables) with a finitely supported probability distribution over several possible outcomes.
One possible outcome is
termination
, which ends execution and either signals success (
\checkmark
) or failure (
), or returns an output value (
{{v}}\in\mathsf{Out}
).
The other possible outcome is
progression
, performing an action (
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
) and transitioning to a state.

Example 3
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
,
\mathsf{At}=\{\alpha,\beta\}
,
\mathsf{Act}=\{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\}
and
\mathsf{Out}=\{v\}
.
On the right, there is a definition of a transition function
\tau:X\times\mathsf{At}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times X)
, while on the left there is a visual representation of
(X,\tau)
.
Given a state
x\in X
and an atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, we write
\tau(x)_{\alpha}
rather than
\tau(x)(\alpha)
.

x_{1}
\circ
\circ
\beta
\alpha
\mathsf{p}\mid{{0.5}}
x_{2}
\mathsf{q}\mid{{0.5}}
\mathsf{q}\mid{{0.5}}
\circ
\alpha,\beta
\checkmark
{{1}}
v
{{0.5}}

\displaystyle\tau(x_{1})_{\alpha}={{\frac{1}{2}}}\delta_{(\mathsf{q},x_{2})}+{{\frac{1}{2}}}\delta_{{{v}}}

\displaystyle\tau(x_{1})_{\beta}={{\frac{1}{2}}}\delta_{(\mathsf{p},x_{1})}+{{\frac{1}{2}}}\delta_{(\mathsf{q},x_{2})}

\displaystyle\tau(x_{2})_{\alpha}=\tau(x_{2})_{\beta}=\delta_{\checkmark}

We use solid lines annotated with (sets of) atoms to denote Boolean guarded branching, dashed lines annotated with atomic actions and probabilities to denote probabilistic labelled transitions to a next state, and double bar arrows pointing at elements of
\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
annotated with probabilities to denote probabilistic transitions that result in termination or output.

The following notions of homomorphism and bisimulation describe structure-preserving maps and relations between
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata.

Definition 4
.

A
homomorphism
between
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
is a function
f:X\to Y
satisfying for all
x\in X
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}

1.

For any
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\gamma(f(x))_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)

2.

For any
(\mathsf{p},y)\in\mathsf{Act}\times Y
,
\gamma(f(x))_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},y)=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times f^{-1}(y)]

Definition 5
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata and let
R\subseteq X\times Y
be a relation.
R
is a
bisimulation
if there exists a transition function
\rho:R\times\mathsf{At}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times R)
such that projection maps
\pi_{1}:R\to X
and
\pi_{2}:R\to Y
given by
\pi_{1}(x,y)=x
and
\pi_{2}(x,y)=y
are homomorphisms from
(R,\rho)
to
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
respectively.

Remark 6
.

Definitions

4
and
5
are direct translations from the coalgebraic theory of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata (see
Appendix

A
).
Coalgebra plays a central role in our proofs, but for purposes of exposition it does not appear in the body of the present paper.

Brzozowski construction.

\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions can be endowed with an operational semantics in the form of a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
\partial:\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathsf{At}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp})
, which we refer to as the
Brzozowski derivative
, as it is reminiscent of the analogous construction for regular expressions and deterministic finite automata due to Brzozowski
[
14
]
.

Given
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
,
{{v}}\in\mathsf{Out}
,
{{r}}\in[0,1]
, and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
, we define

\partial({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\delta_{\checkmark}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\\
\delta_{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\bar{b}}}\end{cases}\partial({{v}})_{\alpha}=\delta_{{{v}}}\quad\quad\quad\quad\partial(\mathsf{p})_{\alpha}=\delta_{(\mathsf{p},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}})}\par\partial(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}={{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}

The derivatives of sequential composition and loops are defined below.
The outgoing transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
depend on whether or not the input atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
satisfies
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
, either outputting
\checkmark
(success) or
(abort) with probability
{{1}}
.
The outgoing transitions of a guarded choice
\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f}
consist of the outgoing transitions of
\mathsf{e}
labelled by atoms satisfying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
and the outgoing transitions of
\mathsf{f}
labelled by atoms satisfying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\bar{b}}}
(as in
\mathsf{GKAT}
).
The output value
v\in\mathsf{Out}
returns the value
v
with probability
{{1}}
given any input atom.
The atomic action
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
emits
\mathsf{p}
given any input atom and transitions to the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
.
The outgoing transitions of the probabilistic choice
\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f}
consist of the outgoing transitions of
\mathsf{e}
with probabilities scaled by
r
and the outgoing transitions of
\mathsf{f}
scaled by
1-r
.

The behaviour of the sequential composition
\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}
is more complicated.
We need to factor in the possibility that
\mathsf{e}
may accept with some probability
{{t}}
given an input atom
\alpha
, in which case the
\alpha
-labelled outgoing transitions of
f
contribute to the outgoing transitions of
\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}
.
Formally, we write
\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}\lhd_{\alpha}\mathsf{f}
, where given
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
we define
(-\lhd_{\alpha}\mathsf{f}):\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp})\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp})
to be the convex extension of
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{f}}:\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp})
given below on the left.

Intuitively,
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{f}}
reroutes the transitions coming out of
\mathsf{e}
: acceptance (the second case) is replaced by the behaviour of
\mathsf{f}
, and the probability mass of transitioning to
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
(the third case) is reassigned to
\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}
.
The branches that output the elements of
\{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.34 6.36 C 2.79 3.92 3.66 2.61 5.35 0.33}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}+\mathsf{Out}
are unchanged by this operation.
A pictorial representation of the effect on the derivatives of
\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}
is given above on the right.
Here, we assume that
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}
can perform a
\mathsf{p}
-transition to
\mathsf{e^{\prime}}
with probability
{{s}}
; we make the same assumption in the informal descriptions of derivatives for loops, below.

For guarded loops, we consider three cases when defining
\partial\left(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)_{\alpha}
.
If
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\bar{b}}}
, then the current state does not satisfy the loop guard and can be skipped:
\partial\left(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)_{\alpha}=\delta_{\checkmark}
.
If
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
and
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
, then the loop body is called, but the inner program
\mathsf{e}
does not perform actions.
We identify divergent loops with rejection and so in this case we set
\partial\left(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)_{\alpha}=\delta_{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.34 6.36 C 2.79 3.92 3.66 2.61 5.35 0.33}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.
If
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{{b}}}
and
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)<{{1}}
, the program executes the loop body and starts again, having to redistribute the probability mass of immediate acceptance
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)
through each execution.
So, for
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{{b}}}
and
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)<{{1}}
, the definition of
\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}
is given below on the left: it rejects or returns when
\mathsf{e}
does, and transitions to
\mathsf{e^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{e}^{(b)}
when
\mathsf{e}
transitions to
\mathsf{e^{\prime}}
.

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(x)}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}&x\in\{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.34 6.36 C 2.79 3.92 3.66 2.61 5.35 0.33}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\frac{\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{e^{\prime}})}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}&x=\left(\mathsf{p},\left(\mathsf{e^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)\right)\\

The reweighing of probabilities used in the definition of the loops comes from defining loops as least fixpoints w.r.t. to an order on distributions, similarly to Stark and Smolka
[
68
]
.

Finally, we specify the behaviour of the probabilistic loop.
In the special case where
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
and
{{r}}={{1}}
, the loop will not terminate; hence we set
\partial\left(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\right)_{\alpha}=\delta_{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.34 6.36 C 2.79 3.92 3.66 2.61 5.35 0.33}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.
In all other cases, we look at
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}
to build
\partial(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]})_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
.
First, we make sure that the loop may be skipped with probability
1-r
.
Next, we account for the possibility that
\mathsf{e}
may reject or return a value, and we modify the productive branches by adding
\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}
to be executed next, as was done for the guarded loop.
The remaining mass is
r\partial(e)_{\alpha}(\checkmark)
, the probability that we will enter the loop with an atom that can skip over the loop body.
As was the case for the guarded loop, we discard this possibility and redistribute it among the remaining branches.
The resulting definition of
\partial(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]})_{\alpha}
is given below on the left.

\displaystyle\partial\left(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\right)_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{{{1-r}}}{{{1}}-{{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}&x=\checkmark\\
\frac{{{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(x)}{{{1}}-{{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}&x\in\{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}

As before, we provide an informal visual depiction of the probabilistic loop semantics above on the right, using the same conventions.

Reachable states.

For any
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
(X,\beta)
and any
x\in X
, we denote by
\langle x\rangle_{\beta}
the set of states reachable from
x
via
\beta
. Clearly,
(\langle x\rangle_{\beta},\beta)
is a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton and is the smallest subautomaton of
(X,\beta)
containing
x
. The canonical inclusion map
(\langle x\rangle_{\beta},\beta)\to(X,\beta)
is a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton homomorphism. In particular,
(\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
is the smallest subautomaton of
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
containing
\mathsf{e}
. We will refer to this subautomaton as the small-step semantics of
\mathsf{e}
. We will often abuse notation and write
\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}
for
(\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
.

The following lemma says that every
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expression generates a finite automaton.

Lemma 7
.

For all
\mathsf{e}\in\mathtt{Exp}
,
\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}
is finite. In fact, the number of states is bounded above by
\#(\mathsf{e}):\mathtt{Exp}\to\mathbb{N}
, where
\#(-)
is defined recursively by

\#({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})=1\hskip 9.24994pt\#({{v}})=1\hskip 9.24994pt\#(\mathsf{p})=2\hskip 9.24994pt\#(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f})=\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})\hskip 9.24994pt\#(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f})=\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})

\#(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})=\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})\hskip 9.24994pt\#\left(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)=\#(\mathsf{e})\hskip 9.24994pt\#\left(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\right)=\#(\mathsf{e})

4
Bisimulations and their properties

Verifying that a given relation is a bisimulation (
Definition

5
) requires that we construct a suitable transition structure on the relation.
In this section, we give necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of such a transition structure.
We also study properties of the bisimilarity relation
\sim
, the largest bisimulation
[
57
]
.

Concrete characterisation of bisimulation equivalence.

There is a beautiful characterisation of bisimulations between Markov chains in
[
36
]
, whose proof makes use of the max-flow min-cut theorem.
Adapting this work to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata produces a useful characterisation of
bisimulation equivalences
, bisimulations that are also equivalence relations.

Lemma 8
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton and let
R\subseteq X\times X
be an equivalence relation.
R
is a bisimulation if and only if and only if for all
(x,y)\in R
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,

1.

for all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(y)_{\alpha}(o)
, and

2.

for all equivalence classes
Q\in X/{R}
and all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{p\}\times Q]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{p\}\times Q]

This lemma can be seen as an extension of Larsen-Skou bisimilarity
[
46
]
to systems with outputs.
Intuitively,
R
is a bisimulation equivalence if for any atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
(x,y)\in R
, the transitions assign the same probabilities to any output, and the probability of transitioning into any given equivalence class after emitting
\mathsf{p}
is the same for both
x
and
y
.

Bisimilarity and its properties.

Given a relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
, define
R^{-1}=\{(y,x)\mid x\mathrel{R}y\}
, and given
A\subseteq X
, write
R(A)=\{y\in Y\mid x\mathrel{R}y,x\in A\}
.
The bisimilarity relation
{\sim_{\beta,\gamma}}\subseteq X\times Y
between
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
is the
greatest fixpoint
of the following operator.

Definition 9
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata and let
R\subseteq X\times Y
.
We define the operator
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}:2^{X\times Y}\to 2^{X\times Y}
so that
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(R)
if for any given
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,

•

for all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(o)
,

•

for all
A\subseteq X
and all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]\leq\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]
, and

•

for all
B\subseteq Y
and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times B]\leq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R^{-1}(B)]
.

From now on, we will omit the subscripts from
\Phi
when the automata are clear from context.

The operator
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}
can also be used to define a behavioural pseudometric.
Let
(X,\beta)
be a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton.
A
relation refinement chain
is an indexed family
\{\sim^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of relations on
X
defined as:

\sim^{(0)}=X\times X
,
\sim^{(i+1)}=\Phi(\sim^{(i)})
.
We can intuitively think of successive elements of this chain as closer approximations of bisimilarity (see also
[
32
]
).

Theorem 10
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton.
For any
x,y\in X
,
x\sim y
if and only if for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
x\sim^{(i)}y
.

Thus, if
x,y\in X
are not bisimilar, then there exists a maximal
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x\sim^{(i)}y
.
In
Section

6
, we use this to define a pseudometric on the states of any
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton.
Informally speaking, this allows us to quantify
how close
to being bisimilar two states are.

Our main goal is to axiomatise bisimilarity of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions with a set of equational laws and reason about equivalence using
equational logic
.
For such an axiomatisation to exist, bisimilarity needs to be both an equivalence relation and a congruence with respect to the
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
operations.
The greatest bisimulation on any
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton is an equivalence
[
57
]
, but being congruence requires an inductive argument.

Theorem 11
.

The greatest bisimulation on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
is a congruence with respect to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
operations.

Table 1:
Axiomatisation of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
. In the figure
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\in\mathtt{Exp}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
,
{{v}}\in\mathsf{Out}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
{{r}},{{s}}\in[0,1]
. Laws involving division of probabilities apply when the denominator is not zero. To simplify the notation, we write
\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)=0
to denote that for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
it holds that
\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}=0
.

Fixpoint Rules

\mathsf{(F1)}

\multirow
3*
\frac{\mathsf{g}\equiv\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{g}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f}\hskip 8.04996pt\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)=0}{\mathsf{g}\equiv\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}}

\mathsf{(F2)}

\multirow
3*
\frac{\mathsf{g}\equiv\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{g}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f}\hskip 8.04996pt\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)=0}{\mathsf{g}\equiv\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}}

Define
\mathsf{E}:\mathtt{Exp}\to\mathsf{At}\to[0,1]
inductively by

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{p}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}\left({{v}}\right)_{\alpha}={{0}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{{1}}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\\
{{0}}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\\
\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}&\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={{r}}\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}+{{(1-r)}}\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{f}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}\left(\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{{0}}&{{r}}={{1}}\text{ and }\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}={{1}}\\
\frac{{{1}}-{{r}}}{{{1}}-{{r}}\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)_{\alpha}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

5
Axiomatisation

We turn our attention to
axiomatisation
of bisimilarity of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions, using an axiom system based on
\mathsf{GKAT}
modulo bisimilarity
[
59
]
.
First, we give an overview of the axioms, and establish their soundness.
Finally, we show that our axioms are strong enough to decompose every expression into a certain syntactic normal form relating the expressions to their small-step semantics.
Completeness is tackled in the next section.

Overview of the axioms.

Table

1
contains the axioms, which are either
equational
(of the form
\mathsf{e}\equiv\mathsf{f}
), or
quasi-equational
(of the form
\mathsf{e}_{1}\equiv\mathsf{f}_{1},\dots\mathsf{e}_{n}\equiv\mathsf{f}_{n}\implies\mathsf{e}\equiv\mathsf{f}
).
It also holds the definition of the function
\mathsf{E}\left(-\right)
, which is necessary to give a side condition to the fixpoint rules.
We define
{\equiv}\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
as the smallest congruence relation satisfying the axioms.

Axioms
\mathsf{G1}
–
\mathsf{G4}
are inherited from
\mathsf{GKAT}
and govern the behaviour of Boolean guarded choice.

\mathsf{P1}
–
\mathsf{P4}
can be thought of as their analogues, but for the probabilistic choice. The distributivity axiom
\mathsf{D}
states that guarded choice distributes over a probabilistic choice, which reflects the way our operational model resolves both types of branching.

The sequencing axioms
\mathsf{S1}
–
\mathsf{S8}
are mostly inherited from
\mathsf{GKAT}
.
The new axioms include
\mathsf{S6}
which talks about right distributivity of sequencing over probabilistic choice and
\mathsf{S7}
which captures the intuitive property that any code executed after a
return
statement is not executed.

\mathsf{L1}
and
\mathsf{L3}
come from
\mathsf{GKAT}
, while
\mathsf{L2}
is a probabilistic loop analogue of
\mathsf{L1}
, which captures the semantics of the probabilistic loop in terms of recursive unrolling.

\mathsf{L4}
equates the
while(true)
and
while(flip(1))
loops.

\mathsf{F1}
and
\mathsf{F2}
are inspired by Salomaa’s axioms
[
58
]
and provide a partial converse to
\mathsf{L1}
and
\mathsf{L2}
respectively, given the loop body cannot immediately terminate.
The property that a loop body has a zero probability of outputting
\checkmark
is formally writen using the side condition
\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{e}\right)=0
, which can be thought of as
empty word property
from Salomaa’s axiomatisation
[
58
]
.

This leaves us with
\mathsf{L5}
and
\mathsf{L6}
, which describe the behaviour of guarded and probabilistic loops where parts of the loop body may be skipped. These are quasi-equational, but can be replaced by equivalent equations — see
Remark

49
.

\mathsf{L5}
concerns a loop on an expression
\mathsf{e}
that has probability
1-{{s}}
of not performing any action, given that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}
holds.
The rule says that, if we start the loop on
\mathsf{e}
given that
\mathsf{c}
holds, then either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
holds and we execute
\mathsf{f}
, or it does not, and the loop is skipped.
The reason that we can disregard the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
part of
\mathsf{e}
is that if this branch is taken, then
\mathsf{c}
still holds on the next iteration of the loop, and so the program will have to choose probabilistically between
\mathsf{f}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
once more.
Since
{{s}}>0
, it will eventually choose the probabilistic branch
\mathsf{f}
with almost sure probability.

The second rule,
\mathsf{L6}
, is the analogue of
\mathsf{L5}
for probabilistic choice.
In this case, however, a choice for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
also means another probabilistic experiment to determine whether the loop needs to be executed once more, with probability
{{r}}
.
The consequence is that if the loop on
\mathsf{e}
is started given that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{c}}
holds, some more probability mass will shift towards skipping, as a result executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
some number of times before halting the loop.

Soundness with respect to bisimilarity.

Using the characterisation from
Section

4
, we can show that
\equiv
is a bisimulation equivalence on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
.
The proof is available in
Appendix

D
.

Lemma 12
.

\equiv
is a bisimulation equivalence on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)

We immediately obtain that provable equivalence is contained in bisimilarity.

Theorem 13

(Soundness)
.

For all
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
, if
\mathsf{e}\equiv\mathsf{f}
then
\mathsf{e}\sim\mathsf{f}

Example of equational reasoning.

Since our axioms are sound, we can reason about
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions equationally, without constructing bisimulations by hand.
Once again, we revisit the algorithm from
Fig.

1
. To show correctness, we need to prove the equivalence of expressions
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}})}
from
Example

1
, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}})}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\left(\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\right)\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\left(\includegraphics[height,viewport,clip]{dice}\oplus_{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}\right)\right)^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{1}})}

\mathsf{S7}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{e}

(Def.
\mathsf{e}

In the second step, we used that for all
\mathsf{e}_{1},\mathsf{e}_{2},\mathsf{e}_{3}\in\mathtt{Exp}
and
{{r}},{{s}}\in[0,1]
with
(1-{{r}})(1-{{s}})>0
, we have
\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus_{{r}}(\mathsf{e}_{2}\oplus_{{{s}}}\mathsf{e}_{3})\equiv(\mathsf{e}_{1}\oplus_{{k}}\mathsf{e}_{2})\oplus_{{l}}\mathsf{e}_{3}
, where
{{k}}={{\frac{r}{1-(1-r)(1-s)}}}
and
{{l}}={{1-(1-r)(1-s)}}
. In the third and eighth steps, we used that for all
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
, we have that
\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}\mathsf{f}\equiv\mathsf{e}
. Both those equivalences follow from the other axioms; see
Lemma

50
in the appendix for details.

Fundamental theorem.

Every expression in the language of
\mathsf{KA}
(resp.
\mathsf{KAT}
,
\mathsf{GKAT}
) can be reconstructed from its small-step semantics, up to
\equiv
.
This property, often referred to as the
fundamental theorem
of (in analogy with the fundamental theorem of calculus and following the terminology of Rutten
[
57
]
) is useful in many contexts, and we will need it later on.

Theorem 14

(Fundamental Theorem)
.

For every
\mathsf{e}\in\mathtt{Exp}
it holds that

\mathsf{e}\equiv\bbigplus\limits_{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}}\left(\bigoplus_{d\in\mathtt{supp}\left(\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}\right)}{\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(d)}\cdot{\mathtt{exp}(d)}\right)

where
\mathtt{exp}
defines a function
\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp}\to\mathtt{Exp}
given by

6
Completeness

Given the axioms presented in the previous section, a natural question is to ask whether they are
complete
w.r.t. bisimilarity — i.e., whether any bisimilar pair can be proved equivalent using the axioms that make up
\equiv
.
The traditional strategy is to develop the idea of
systems of equations
within the calculus, and show that these systems have unique (least) solutions up to provable equivalence.
If we can characterise the expressions of interest as solutions to a common system of equations (typically derived from the bisimulation that relates them), then uniqueness of solutions guarantees their equivalence.
Unfortunately, the first step of this process, where systems of equations are shown to have unique solutions, does not transfer to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
(nor
\mathsf{GKAT}
).
Indeed, some systems of equations do not have
any
solution
[
45
,
59
]
; the lack of a procedure to construct solutions also encumbers a proof of uniqueness.

Instead, we follow the approach from
[
64
]
pioneered by Bergstra and Klop
[
11
]
, and incorporate uniqueness of solutions into the axiomatisation.
The
uniqueness axiom
(
\mathsf{UA}
) that accomplishes this is an
axiom scheme
, which is to say it is a template for infinitely many axioms, one for each number of unknowns.
In the case of a single unknown, one can show that
\mathsf{F1}
and
\mathsf{F2}
are special cases of
\mathsf{UA}
, which moreover give a candidate solution.

With
\mathsf{UA}
in hand, the traditional roadmap towards completeness works out.
Before we get there, however, we must expend some energy to properly state this axiom scheme.
Moreover, showing soundness of
\mathsf{UA}
requires effort.
Both of these take up the bulk of the development in this section; we derive the desired completeness property at the end.

(Salomaa) systems of equations.

First, we define formally the idea of
systems of equations
for
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata.
The constraints on each variable will be built using the following two-sorted grammar, where
X
is a finite set of indeterminates.

\displaystyle e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathtt{Exp}(X)

\displaystyle::=p\mid e_{1}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}e_{2}

(
p\in\mathtt{PExp}(X),{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
)

\displaystyle p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathtt{PExp}(X)

\displaystyle::=\mathsf{f}\mid\mathsf{g}x\mid p_{1}\oplus_{{{r}}}p_{2}

(
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\in\mathtt{Exp},x\in X,{{r}}\in[0,1]
)

Definition 15
.

A
system of equations
is a pair
(X,\tau:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X))
consisting of a finite set
X
of indeterminates and a function
\tau:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X)
.
If for all
x\in X
, in each of
\tau(x)
all subterms of the form
\mathsf{g}x
satisfy
\mathsf{E}\left(\mathsf{g}\right)=0
, then such system is called
Salomaa
.
1
1

1

In process algebra
[
52
]
, Salomaa systems are usually called
guarded
.
We avoid the latter name to prevent confusion with Boolean guarded choice present in
\mathsf{(Prob)GKAT}
.

Every finite state
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton yields a Salomaa system of equations.

Definition 16
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be a finite state
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton. A system of equations associated with
(X,\beta)
is a Salomaa system
(X,\tau)
, with
\tau:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X)
defined by

\tau(x)=\bbigplus\limits_{\alpha\in\mathsf{At}}\left(\bigoplus_{d\in\mathtt{supp}(\beta(x))_{\alpha}}\beta(x)_{\alpha}(d)\cdot\mathtt{sys}(d)\right)

where
\mathtt{sys}:\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathtt{Exp}\to\mathtt{PExp}(X)
is given by

\mathtt{sys}(\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
