Example 17
.

In the system associated with the automaton from
Example

3
,
\tau
is given by

x_{1}\mapsto(\mathsf{q}x_{2}\oplus_{{\frac{1}{2}}}{{v}})+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\alpha}}\left((\mathsf{p}x_{1}\oplus_{{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathsf{q}x_{2})+_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\beta}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}\right)x_{2}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\alpha}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}+_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\beta}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}\right)

Given a function
h:X\to\mathtt{Exp}
that assigns a value to each indeterminate in
X
, we can derive a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expression
h^{\#}(e)
for each
e\in\mathtt{Exp}(X)
inductively, as follows:
h^{\#}(\mathsf{f})=\mathsf{f}
,
h^{\#}(p_{1}\oplus_{{r}}p_{2})=h^{\#}(p_{1})\oplus_{{r}}h^{\#}(p_{2})
,
h^{\#}(\mathsf{g}x)=\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}h(x)
,
h^{\#}(e_{1}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}e_{2})=h^{\#}(e_{1})+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}h^{\#}(e_{2})
. We can now state the notion of a
solution
to the Salomaa system.
Rather than expecting both sides of equations to be strictly equal, we require them to be related by a relation, which we leave as a parameter to instantiate later.

Definition 18
.

Let
R\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
.
A
solution
up to
R
to a system
(X,\tau)
is a map
h:X\to\mathtt{Exp}
satisfying for all
x\in X
that
\left(h(x),h^{\#}(\tau(x))\right)\in R
.

Example 19
.

A solution up to
\equiv
to the system from
Example

17
would satisfy

h(x_{1})\equiv(\mathsf{q}\mathbin{;}h(x_{2})\oplus_{{\frac{1}{2}}}{{v}})+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\alpha}}\left((\mathsf{p}\mathbin{;}h(x_{1})\oplus_{{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathsf{q}\mathbin{;}h(x_{2}))+_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\beta}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}\right)h(x_{2})\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\alpha}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}+_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\beta}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{0}}}\right)

In this case, choosing
h(x_{1})=(\mathsf{p}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\beta}}{{v}})^{[{{\frac{1}{2}}}]}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{q}
and
h(x_{2})={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{\mathsf{1}}}
fits these constraints.

Example 20
.

Let
\mathsf{r}\in[0,1]
. The recursive specification on the left below describes a program
\mathsf{randAdd(m,r)}
which takes an integer
\mathsf{m}
and bias
\mathsf{r}
.
As long as
\mathsf{m}
is strictly below
10
, this program flips an
\mathsf{r}
-biased coin to decide between incrementing
\mathsf{m}
followed by a recursive call or termination. That recursive specification can be thought of as a Salomaa system with one unknown; the program on the right is a solution up to
\equiv
.

def

\mathsf{randAdd(m,r)}
:

if

\mathsf{m<10}

then

if

\mathsf{flip(r)}

then

\mathsf{m}\texttt{++}
;

\mathsf{randAdd(m,r)}
else

skip

else

skip

while

\mathsf{flip(r)}

do

if

\mathsf{m<10}

then

\mathsf{m}\texttt{++}
;

\mathsf{randAdd(m,r)}
else

skip

Solutions up to
\equiv
can be characterised concretely, using
Theorem

14
.

Theorem 21
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be a finite state
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton. The map
h:X\to\mathtt{Exp}
is a solution up to
\equiv
to the system associated with
(X,\beta)
if and only if
[-]_{\equiv}\circ h
is a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata homomorphism from
(X,\beta)
to
({\mathtt{Exp}}/{\equiv},\bar{\partial})
. We write
\bar{\partial}
to denote the unique transition function on
{\mathtt{Exp}}/{\equiv}
which makes the quotient map
[-]_{\equiv}:\mathtt{Exp}\to{\mathtt{Exp}}/{\equiv}
a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton homomorphism from
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)

[
57
, Proposition 5.8]
.

Uniqueness of Solutions axiom.

Informally,
\mathsf{UA}
extends
\equiv
by stating that solutions to Salomaa systems, if they exist, are unique.
Formally, we define
{\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}}\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
to be the least congruence that contains
\equiv
, and satisfies the following (quasi-equational) axiom:

\frac{(X,\tau)\text{ is a Salomaa system}\quad f,g:X\to\mathtt{Exp}\text{ are solutions of }(X,\tau)\text{ up to }\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}}{f(x)\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}g(x)\text{ for all }x\in X}

(
\mathsf{UA}
)

\mathsf{F1}
and
\mathsf{F2}
are instantiations of
\mathsf{UA}
for Salomaa systems with one variable.

Behavioural pseudometric.

We now develop the theory necessary to verify soundness of
\mathsf{UA}
.
First, note that for every
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
, we can define a function
d_{X}:X\times X\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
:

d_{X}(x,y)=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&n\text{ is maximal such that }x\sim^{(n)}y\\
0&\text{if }x\sim y\end{cases}

The above is well-defined by
Theorem

10
, and is a
pseudometric
, in the following sense.

Definition 22
.

A
pseudometric space
is a pair
(X,d_{X})
, where
d_{X}:X\times X\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a
pseudometric
, which means that for all
x,y,z\in X
we have

d_{X}(x,x)=0d_{X}(x,y)=d_{X}(y,x)d_{X}(x,z)\leq d_{X}(x,y)+d_{X}(y,z)

Let
k\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. A mapping
f:X\to Y
between pseudometric spaces
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
is called
k
-Lipschitz
if for all
x,y\in X

d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq kd_{X}(x,y)
.

The behavioural pseudometric satisfies the definition above, in a strong sense.

Lemma 23
.

For every
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton,
(X,\beta)
,
(X,d_{X})
a pseudometric space that is
ultra
, in the sense that for all
x,y,z\in X
we have
d_{X}(x,z)=\max\{d_{X}(x,y),d_{X}(y,z)\}
.

Let
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
be pseudometric spaces.
Their
product
is a pseudometric space
(X\times Y,d_{X\times Y})
where
d_{X\times Y}
is defined by
d_{X\times Y}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\max\{d_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),d_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})\}
.
It is easy to show that if both pseudometric spaces are ultra, then so is their product.
Going forward, we will omit subscripts when they are clear from context.

Soundness of the Uniqueness Axiom.

Every Salomaa system
(X,\tau:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X))
with
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\}
induces a mapping
\bar{\tau}:\mathtt{Exp}^{n}\to\mathtt{Exp}^{n}
.
Intuitively, this mapping takes a vector
\vec{\mathsf{e}}=(\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{n})
, and produces a new vector where the
i
-th component is the evaluation of
\tau(x_{i})
when each
x_{j}
is substituted by
\mathsf{e}_{j}
.
More formally, given this
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
, we define
e:X\to\mathtt{Exp}
by
e(x_{i})=\mathsf{e}_{i}
, and set
\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{e}})=\left((e^{\#}\circ\tau)(x_{1}),\dots,(e^{\#}\circ\tau)(x_{n})\right)
.

To establish soundness of
\mathsf{UA}
, we first show that
\bar{\tau}
is
\frac{1}{2}
-Lipschitz on the pseudometric space
(\mathtt{Exp}^{n},d)
, where
d
is the metric that arises from the
n
-fold product of
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
.

Lemma 24
.

Given a Salomaa system
(X,\tau:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X))
, the map
\bar{\tau}:\mathtt{Exp}^{n}\to\mathtt{Exp}^{n}
from the pseudometric space
(\mathtt{Exp}^{n},d)
to itself is
\frac{1}{2}
-Lipschitz.

Finally, we can prove the following.

Lemma 25
.

\mathsf{UA}
is satisfied by bisimilarity.

Proof.

Let
(X,\tau)
is a Salomaa system, with
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, and let
f,g:X\to\mathtt{Exp}(X)
be solutions up to
\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}
to the system.
Finally, let
\vec{\mathsf{f}}=(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n}))
and
\vec{\mathsf{g}}=(g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{n}))
.
Assume that the premises are satisfied by the bisimilarity,
f(x_{i})\sim(f^{\#}\circ\tau)(x_{i})
and
g(x_{i})\sim(g^{\#}\circ\tau)(x_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
In other words, we have that
d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\vec{\mathsf{f}})=0
and
d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{g}}),\vec{\mathsf{g}})=0

Let
d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{g}}))=k
for some
k\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Then, since
d
is ultra,

d(\vec{\mathsf{f}},\vec{\mathsf{g}})=\max\{d(\vec{\mathsf{f}},\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}})),d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\vec{\mathsf{g}})\}=d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\vec{\mathsf{g}})=\max\{d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{g}})),d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{g}}),\vec{\mathsf{g}})\}=k

By
Lemma

24
, we find

k=d(\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{f}}),\bar{\tau}(\vec{\mathsf{g}}))\leq\frac{1}{2}d(\vec{\mathsf{f}},\vec{\mathsf{g}})=\frac{1}{2}k

which implies that

d(\vec{\mathsf{f}},\vec{\mathsf{g}})=0
. Because of the definition of the product pseudometric on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
, we have

f(x_{i})\sim g(x_{i})

for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Therefore, the conclusion of the
\mathsf{UA}
is satisfied by bisimilarity.
∎

Because of the above lemma and
Theorem

13
, both
\mathsf{UA}
and the axioms of
\equiv
are contained in
\sim
, the greatest bisimulation on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
. Recall that
\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}
is the least congruence containing those rules. Since
\sim
on
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
is a congruence (
Theorem

11
), we have that
\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}
is sound.

Theorem 26

(Soundness with
\mathsf{UA}
)
.

For all
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
if
\mathsf{e}\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}\mathsf{f}
then
\mathsf{e}\sim\mathsf{f}
.

Completeness.

After all the hard work is done, the proof of completeness follows via the same line of reasoning as the one for
\mathsf{GKAT}

[
64
,
59
]
.

Theorem 27

(Completeness)
.

For all
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
if
\mathsf{e}\sim\mathsf{f}
then
\mathsf{e}\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}\mathsf{f}

Proof.

Let
R\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
be a bisimulation with a transition structure
\rho:R\times\mathsf{At}\to\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times R)
relating
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata
(\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
and
(\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
such that
(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f})\in R
.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be the projection homomorphisms from
(R,\rho)
to
(\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
and
(\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
respectively.
Since both
\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}
and
\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}
are finite (by
Lemma

7
), so is
R
.

Let
j,k
be the inclusion homomorphisms of
(\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
and
(\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial},\partial)
in
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
.
We can construct two homomorphisms
[-]_{\equiv}\circ j\circ\pi_{1}
and
[-]_{\equiv}\circ k\circ\pi_{2}
from
(R,\rho)
to
(\mathtt{Exp}/{\equiv},\bar{\partial})
.
By
Theorem

21
,
j\circ\pi_{1}
and
k\circ\pi_{2}
are solutions up to
\equiv
to the Salomaa system associated with
(R,\rho)
.
Since
\equiv
is contained in
\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}
, those are immediately also solutions up to
\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}
.

Because of
\mathsf{UA}
, we have that
(j\circ\pi_{1})(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}(k\circ\pi_{2})(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})
for all
(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})\in R
.
Thus,

\mathsf{e}\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}j(\mathsf{e})\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}(j\circ\pi_{1})(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f})\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}(k\circ\pi_{2})(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f})\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}k(\mathsf{f})\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}\mathsf{f}\qed

7
Decidability and Complexity

To decide whether
\mathsf{e}\mathrel{\dot{\equiv}}\mathsf{f}
, we need to demonstrate the existence of a bisimulation between the states
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{f}
in
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
.
Since bisimulations need only involve reachable states, it suffices to find this bisimulation within
\langle\mathsf{e,f}\rangle_{\partial}
, the smallest subautomaton of
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
containing
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{f}
, which is also the union of
\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}
and
\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}
; this automaton is finite by
Lemma

7
.
We thus focus on the problem of deciding bisimilarity within a single finite
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton.

Our analysis in this section is facilitated by two simplifying assertions.

1.

To avoid having to compare real (infinite-precision) probabilities, we limit
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions to rational probabilities
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
in this section.
This restriction is compatible with the earlier operators on probabilities, which all preserve rationality.

2.

Equivalence of
\mathsf{GKAT}
proper is co-NP-hard
[
64
]
, simply because Boolean unsatisfiability can trivially be encoded in the language of tests.
We take a fixed-parameter approach, assuming that
\mathsf{At}
, the set of atoms that can appear on transitions, is fixed beforehand.

Coalgebraic partition refinement.

We rely on
partition refinement

[
37
,
38
,
55
]
, which effectively computes the
largest bisimulation
on an automaton, by approximating it from above.
In the
coalgebraic
presentation of partition refinement
[
73
]
, which we instantiate to our setting, automata of various types are encoded as abstract graphs.
More specifically, an automaton is encoded in two maps
o:X\to O
and
\ell:X\to\mathcal{B}(L\times X)
, where

•

X
is a set of
nodes
that represent (partial) states of the automaton;

•

O
is a set of
observable values
at each node;

•

L
is a set representing possible
labels
of edges between nodes;

•

\mathcal{B}(L\times X)
is a multiset of pairs representing
edges
between nodes.

Subject to a number of coherence conditions on the encoding (omitted here), coalgebraic partition refinement yields an
\mathcal{O}(n\log|X|)
algorithm to compute the largest bisimulation on an automaton, where
n=\sum_{x\in X}|\ell(x)|
is the number of
edges
of the automaton.

Encoding
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata.

Coalgebraic partition refinement provides suitable encodings for well-known transition types, as well as methods to soundly obtain encodings of composite transition types
[
73
]
.
The details of these techniques are beyond the scope of this paper, but the underlying idea is fairly intuitive: composite transition types are encoded by inserting synthetic nodes that represent partially evaluated states — not unlike how our drawings contain intermediate nodes that are the target of
\alpha
-labelled arrows.
More precisely, the nodes of an encoded
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
(Q,t)
are three-sorted:

1.

every state of the automaton is a node; and

2.

every “intermediate” state (the small circles in our drawings) is a node; and

3.

every probabilistic edge gives rise to another node.

Nodes of the third kind separate the dashed arrows in our drawings (labelled with a probability as well as an action) into two arrows, each of which is labelled by one value.

Formally, we choose
X:=Q+I+\mathsf{Act}\times Q
as our set of nodes, where
I:=\{t(q)_{\alpha}:q\in Q,\alpha\in\mathsf{At}\}
.
We also set
L:=\mathsf{At}+\mathbb{Q}+\mathsf{Act}
.
The map
\ell:X\to\mathcal{B}(L\times X)
is then defined by:
2
2

2

Here,
\{\!|-\mid-|\!\}
denotes multiset comprehension, where each element occurs at most once.

\ell(x):=\begin{cases}\{\!|(\alpha,t(q)_{\alpha})\mid\alpha\in\mathsf{At}|\!\}&x=q\in Q\\
\{\!|(d(\mathsf{p},q),(\mathsf{p},q))\mid\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act},q\in Q|\!\}&x=d\in I\\
\{\!|(\mathsf{p},q)|\!\}&x=(\mathsf{p},q)\in\mathsf{Act}\times Q\end{cases}

In other words,
\ell
labels the edges between nodes of the first and second kind with an atom, the edges between nodes of the second and third kind with a probability, and the edges between nodes of the third and first kind with an action.

Observables represent the probabilities assigned to acceptance, rejection, or a return value by nodes of the second kind.
Formally,
O:=1+\mathbb{Q}^{2+\mathsf{Out}}
, where
*\in 1
means “no observable value”, and values from
\mathbb{Q}^{2+\mathsf{Out}}
assign a probability to each
\xi\in 2+\mathsf{Out}
.
We can then define
o:X\to O
by setting
o(d)(\xi):=d(\xi)
when
d\in I
, and
o(x):=*
otherwise.
3
3

3

If the coalgebraic approach from
[
73
]
is followed to the letter, the observable map for nodes of the third kind behaves slightly differently; we simplify our encoding here for the sake of presentation.

Deciding bisimilarity.

We can now leverage the encoding given above to decide bisimilarity.

Theorem 28
.

If all probabilities are rational and
\mathsf{At}
is fixed, then bisimilarity of states in a
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automaton
(Q,t)
is decidable in time
\mathcal{O}(|Q|^{2}|\mathsf{Act}|\log(|\mathsf{Act}\times Q|))
.

Proof.

The results from
[
73
]
ensure that our encoding of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata can be equipped with an appropriate interface that allows their algorithm to decide equivalence.

As for the complexity, we instantiate their abstract complexity result by computing the parameters.
The number of nodes and edges can be bound from above fairly easily, as follows:

\displaystyle|X|

\displaystyle=|Q|+|I|+|\mathsf{Act}\times Q|\leq|Q|+2\cdot|\mathsf{Act}|\cdot|Q|

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}|\ell(x)|\leq|Q|\cdot|\mathsf{At}|+|Q|^{2}\cdot|\mathsf{Act}|+|Q|\cdot|\mathsf{Act}|

Since
\mathsf{At}
is fixed, the claimed complexity then follows.
∎

This allows us to conclude that bisimilarity of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expressions is also decidable.

Corollary 29
.

If all probabilities are rational and
\mathsf{At}
is fixed, then
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
equivalence of
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
is decidable in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\log n)
, where
n=\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

7
,
\langle\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}
is of size at most
n
, and the number of distinct actions
\mathsf{e}
or
\mathsf{f}
is fixed from above by
n
as well.
The claim then follows by
Theorem

28
.
∎

8
Related work

Our work builds on
\mathsf{GKAT}
, a strictly deterministic fragment
[
45
]
of Kleene Algebra with Tests (
\mathsf{KAT}
).

\mathsf{KAT}
has been used in several verification tasks, such as cache control
[
17
]
, compiler optimisations
[
43
]
, source-to-source translations
[
3
]
, and network properties
[
2
,
25
,
24
,
65
,
66
,
72
]
, and was generalised to include fuzzy logics
[
26
]
.

\mathsf{GKAT}
admits a Salomaa-style
[
58
]
axiomatisation of trace equivalence
[
64
]
and bisimilarity
[
59
]
, both relying on the Uniqueness of Solutions axiom, and completeness without it remains open, though completeness of a fragment of
\mathsf{GKAT}
was recently proved by
[
39
]
.

\mathsf{GKAT}
modulo bisimilarity and Milner’s interpretation of regular expressions arise as fragments of the
parametrised processes
framework
[
61
]
; this is not the case for
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
due to a different treatment of loops.
The uniqueness axiom was originally introduced by Bergstra and Klop under the name Recursive Specification Principle (
\mathsf{RSP}
)
[
11
]
and used in axiomatisations of process calculi
[
12
]
. The general pattern of their proofs of completeness is similar to ours, although the key challenge is the extension to the probabilistic setting.

Our paper also builds up the vast line of research on probabilistic bisimulation
[
46
,
62
,
21
]
and the coalgebraic approach to systems with probabilistic transitions
[
19
,
9
,
21
,
67
]
.
More concretely, we relied on relation refinement characterisation of bisimilarity
[
69
]
, natural metrics on the final coalgebras for
\omega
-accessible endofunctors
[
8
,
75
]
, coalgebraic completeness theorems
[
33
,
63
,
60
]
and minimisation algorithms for coalgebras
[
73
,
20
,
74
,
35
]
.
Axiomatisations of probabilistic bisimulation were extensively studied in the process algebra community, including a recursion-free process algebra of Bandini and Segala
[
7
]
and recursive calculi of Stark and Smolka
[
68
]
and Mislove, Ouaknine and Worrell
[
53
]
. Aceto, Ésik and Ingólfsdóttir
[
1
]
gave an alternative axiomatisation of Stark and Smolka’s calculus by extending Iteration Theories
[
13
,
23
]
with equational axioms.

Probabilistic Kleene Algebra (
\mathsf{pKA}
)
[
49
]
relaxes the axioms of
\mathsf{KA}
to accommodate reasoning about probabilistic predicate transformers; its axioms are complete w.r.t. simulation equivalence of NFAs
[
50
]
.

\mathsf{pKA}
was also extended with a probabilistic choice operator and concurrency primitives
[
51
]
, but completeness this system was not considered.

\mathsf{ProbNetKAT}

[
24
,
66
]
is a domain-specific language for reasoning about probabilistic effects in networks based on
\mathsf{KAT}
, which features a probabilistic choice operator, however, axiomatisation of the obtained language was not studied.

9
Conclusion and Future Work

We have presented
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
, a language for reasoning about uninterpreted programs with branching and loops, with both Boolean and probabilistic guards.
We provided an automata-theoretic operational model and characterised bisimilarity for these automata.
We gave a sound and complete axiomatisation of bisimilarity, relying on the Uniqueness of Solutions (
\mathsf{UA}
) axiom, and showed bisimilarity can be efficiently decided in
O(n^{3}\log n)
time.

A first natural direction for future work is the question whether the more traditional language semantics of
\mathsf{GKAT}
can be lifted to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
and axiomatised.
More broadly, we would like to investigate notions of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
expression equivalence more permissive than bisimilarity, including the notion of
bisimulation distance

[
6
]
and its possible axiomatisations based on
quantitative equational logic

[
48
,
4
]
.

A second direction touches on the problem of completeness without
\mathsf{UA}
, which is still open for
\mathsf{(Prob)GKAT}
.
In light of recent completeness results for the skip-free fragment of
\mathsf{GKAT}

[
39
]
modulo bisimilarity and trace equivalence, we are interested to study the skip-free fragment of
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
.
The proofs in
[
39
]
do not immediately generalise to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
as probabilities do not obviously embed into (1-free) regular expressions.

Similarly to
\mathsf{GKAT}
,
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
is strictly deterministic and thus avoids known complications of combining nondeterminism with probabilistic choice
[
36
,
71
,
27
]
.
We are interested if the recent work on combining multisets and probabilities via distributive laws
[
34
,
44
]
could be applied to extending our developments with nondeterminism.

\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
can express only uninterpreted programs, hence it cannot be used to reason about programs involving mutable state. An example of a probabilistic program with state is Pólya’s urn
[
47
]
. One way of adding mutable state
[
28
]
to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
is by adding
hypotheses

[
16
]
. Unfortunately, adding hypotheses can lead to undecidability or incompleteness
[
41
]
, although there are forms of hypotheses that retain completeness
[
42
,
22
,
56
]
and exploring this is as an interesting direction for future work.

Appendix A
Coalgebra

In the main text of the paper, we avoided using the language of universal coalgebra
[
57
,
29
]
in the presentation, so as not to distract from the main concepts, which can be described concretely.
We have however used coalgebra in our development, and concrete definitions in the main text are instances of abstract notions.
This is helpful in simplifying proofs, so in the appendix, we will present the proofs of the results using coalgebra.

We assume that the reader is familiar with the basic notions of category theory, such as functors, pullbacks and natural transformations.
We first recall the basic notions from universal coalgebra; for a more detailed introduction, we refer to
[
57
,
29
]
.

Definition 30
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
a
\mathtt{Set}
-endofunctor. A
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebra
is a pair
(X,\beta)
where
X
is a set and
\beta:X\to\mathcal{B}X
is a
transition function
.
A homomorphism between two
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebras
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
is a function
h:X\to Y
satisfying
\mathcal{B}h\circ\beta=\gamma\circ h
.

\mathcal{B}
-coalgebras and homomorphisms between them form a category, which we denote
\mathsf{Coalg}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Recall that the set
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(X)
of finitely supported probability distributions is an endofunctor on
\mathtt{Set}
.
As alluded to before,
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata can modelled as coalgebras for the functor
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}+\mathsf{Act}\times\mathsf{Id})^{\mathsf{At}}
, and we will study them as such going forward.

Definition 31
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebras, with
Y\subseteq X
. If the inclusion
i:Y\to X
is a
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebra homomorphism, then
(Y,\gamma)
is called a
subcoalgebra
of
(X,\beta)
.

There can be at most one coalgebra structure map
\gamma:Y\to\mathcal{B}Y
that makes the inclusion
i:Y\to X
a
B
-coalgebra homomorphism from
(Y,\gamma)
to
(X,\beta)

[
57
]
.
Subcoalgebras of any
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebra
(X,\beta)
form a complete lattice
[
57
]
.
Given
x\in X
, we will write
\langle x\rangle_{\beta}
for the smallest subcoalgebra of
(X,\beta)
containing
x
, the
subcoalgebra generated by
x
.

Definition 32
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras.
A relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
is a
bisimulation
if there exists a function
\rho:R\to\mathcal{B}R
such that the projections
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:R\to X
are
\mathcal{B}
-coalgebra homomorphisms from
(R,\rho)
to
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
respectively.
We say the elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
are
bisimilar
if there exists a bisimulation
R
such that
(x,y)\in R

Note that
Definition

4
is an instantiation of the abstract definition of coalgebra homomorphism from
Definition

30
to
\mathcal{G}
.
Similarly,
Definition

5
is an instantiation of
Definition

32
.

Proposition 33
.

1.

The functor
\mathcal{G}
is bounded and preserves weak pullbacks

2.

There exists a
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra
(Z,\zeta)
which is a final object in
\mathsf{Coalg}_{\mathcal{G}}
. In other words, for any
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra
(X,\beta)
there exists a unique homomorphism
!_{\beta}:X\to Z

3.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras. The elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
are bisimilar if and only if
{!_{\beta}(x)}={!_{\gamma}(y)}

Proof.

(1) It was proved by Moss in
[
54
]
that
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
preserves weak pullbacks.
Gumm and Schröder
[
30
]
showed that preservation of weak pullbacks is closed under functor composition, products and coproducts.
Since the
\mathtt{Set}
endofunctors
F(X)=X^{A}
,
G(X)=B+X
and
H(X)=C
(where
A,B
and
C
are arbitrary sets) preserve weak pullbacks, so does
\mathcal{G}
.

(2) The functor
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
is known to be bounded by
\omega

[
19
, Theorem 4.6]
.
Since
\mathsf{Act}
and
\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
are finite, they are also
\omega
-bounded.
Boundedness is preserved under functor composition, binary products and binary coproducts
[
31
, Corollary 4.9]
.
Since
\mathsf{At}
is finite, the exponential functor
\mathsf{Id}^{\mathsf{At}}
is also bounded.
Altogether, we can conclude that
\mathcal{G}
is bounded.
Existence of the final coalgebra follows from boundedness
[
57
, Theorem 10.3]
.

(3) Follows from the fact that
\mathcal{G}
preserves weak pullbacks
[
57
, Theorem 9.3]
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs from
Section

3

See
7

Proof.

We adapt the analogous proof for
\mathsf{GKAT}

[
59
]
. For any
\mathsf{e}\in\mathtt{Exp}
, let
|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|
be the cardinality of the carrier set of the least subcoalgebra of
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
containing
\mathsf{e}
. We show by induction that for all
\mathsf{e}\in\mathtt{Exp}
it holds that
|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq\#(\mathsf{e})
.

For the base cases, observe that for
{{v}}\in\mathsf{Out}
the generated subcoalgebra has exactly one state, which outputs the appropriate value with probability
1
. Hence,
\#({{v}})=1
. Similarly, for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
, we have
\#({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})=1

For
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
, we have two states; the initial state, which transitions with probability
1
on
\mathsf{p}
to the state which outputs
\checkmark
with probability
1
.

For the inductive cases, assume that
|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq\#(e)
,
|\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq\#(f)
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
and
{{r}}\in[0,1]
.

•

Every derivative of
\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f}
is either a derivative of
\mathsf{e}
or
\mathsf{f}
and hence
|\langle\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|+|\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}|=\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})=\#(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f})
.
By analogous reasoning,
|\langle\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{{r}}}\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq\#(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f})
.

•

In the case of
\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}
, every derivative of this expression is either a derivative of
\mathsf{f}
or some derivative of
\mathsf{e}
followed by
\mathsf{f}
. Hence,
|\langle\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}\rangle_{\partial}|=|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}\times\{\mathsf{f}\}|+|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq\#(\mathsf{e})+\#(\mathsf{f})=\#(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})
.

•

For the probabilistic loop case, observe that every derivative of
\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}
is a derivative of
\mathsf{e}
followed by
\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}
. It can be easily observed, that there is as many derivatives of
\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}
as derivatives of
\mathsf{e}
. Hence,
|\langle\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]}\rangle_{\partial}|\leq|\langle\mathsf{e}\rangle_{\partial}|=\#(\mathsf{e})=\#(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]})
. We omit the case of guarded loop, as reasoning is identical to the case of the probabilistic loop.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs from section
Section

4

A
flow network
is a pair
(G,c)
where
G=(V,E)
is a directed graph, and the edges
E\subseteq V\times V
are equipped with a capacity function
c:E\to\mathbb{R}^{+}_{\infty}
.
An
(s,t)
-flow
through a network
(G,c)
where
s,t\in V
is a function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}^{+}_{\infty}
satisfying the following:

1.

For all
v\in V\setminus\{s,t\}

\sum_{(u,v)\in E}f(u,v)=\sum_{(v,u)\in E}f(v,u)

2.

For all
e\in E
,
f(e)\leq c(e)
(Admissibility of the flow)

3.

There exists a constant
F\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{\infty}
called the value of the flow, satisfying

F=\sum_{(s,u)\in E}f(s,u)=\sum_{(v,t)\in E}f(v,t)

Let
S\subseteq V
, such that
s\in S
.
An
(s,t)
-cut
is the set of edges
E(S,V\setminus S)=\{(u,v)\in E\mid u\in S,v\in V\setminus S\}
.
The
capacity
of the cut is given by

C_{S}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u,v)\in E(S,V\setminus S)\end{subarray}}c(u,v)

The following result about flows and cuts is well known.

Theorem 34

(Max-flow min-cut theorem
[
18
]
)
.

Let
(G,c)
be a flow network with
G=(V,E)
and let
s,t\in V
be vertices. The maximum value of an admissible
(s,t)
-flow equals the minimum capacity of any
(s,t)
-cut.

With this in hand, we can verify our characterisation of bisimulations.

Lemma 35
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
automata and let
R\subseteq X\times Y
be a relation.
R
is a bisimulation if and only if for all
(x,y)\in R
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, the following hold.

1.

For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(o)

2.

For all
A\subseteq X
and all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]\leq\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

3.

For all
B\subseteq Y
and all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times B]\leq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R^{-1}(B)]

Proof.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras and let
R\subseteq X\times Y
be a relation.

Definition

32
states that
R
is a bisimulation if and only if there exists a
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra structure map
\rho:R\to\mathcal{G}R
, which makes the canonical projection maps into homomorphsims from
(R,\rho)
to
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
respectively.
We can work out this definition more concretely.
Namely, relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
is a bisimulation if and only if there exists
\mathcal{G}
coalgebra structure map
\rho:R\to\mathcal{G}R
, such that for all
(x,y)\in R
and all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, the following hold:

1.

For all
o\in 2+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(o)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(o)

2.

For all
(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Act}\times X

\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R}\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))

3.

For all
(\mathsf{p},y^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Act}\times Y

\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},y^{\prime})=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R}\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))

Condition (1) of the above definition is readily equivalent to the condition (1) of ths lemma.

We now show that existence of the coalgebra structure map
\rho:R\to\mathcal{G}R
satisfying the latter conditions is equivalent to conditions of this lemma, by constructing a family of flow networks and employing the max-flow min-cut theorem.
Assume
(x,y)\in R
.
For each
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
p\in\mathsf{Act}
, construct a flow network
(G_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}},c_{\alpha,p})
where
G_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}=(V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}},E_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})
, as follows.

\displaystyle V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}={}

\displaystyle\{s,t\}\cup\{x^{\prime}\in X\mid\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})>0\}\cup\{y^{\prime}\in Y\mid\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},y^{\prime})>0\}

\displaystyle E_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}={}

\displaystyle\{(s,x^{\prime})\mid x^{\prime}\in X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}\}

\displaystyle{}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mid x^{\prime}\in(X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}),y^{\prime}\in(Y\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R\}

\displaystyle{}\cup\{(y^{\prime},t)\mid y^{\prime}\in Y\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}\}

Informally speaking,
V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
contains the designated source and target vertices
s
and
t
, as well as the support of
\beta(x)_{\alpha}
and
\gamma(y)_{\alpha}
restricted to tuples having
\mathsf{p}
in the first coordinate.

We add an edge from
s
(resp.
t
) to any element in the support of
\beta(x)_{\alpha}
(resp.
\gamma(y)_{\alpha}
) contained in the set of vertices.
Vertices are connected when they are related by
R
.

The capacity function
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
is defined as follows: for all
x^{\prime}\in X\cap V_{\alpha,p}
we set
c_{\alpha,p}(s,x^{\prime})=\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})
and for all
y^{\prime}\in Y\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
we set
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(y^{\prime},t)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},y^{\prime})
. Otherwise, for the remaining
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in E
, we choose
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=+\infty
.

First, assume that conditions (1–3) of the lemma hold. Let
S\subseteq V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
be the set containing
s
such that
C=E(S,V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}\setminus S)
is the minimal capacity cut.
This set cannot contain the central edges of infinite capacity, as otherwise the corresponding flow would not be admissible.

Without loss of generality,
C=A\cup B
where
A=\{(s,x^{\prime})\mid s\in S,x^{\prime}\in V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}\setminus S\}
and
B=\{(y^{\prime},t)\mid y^{\prime}\in S,t\in V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}\setminus S\}
. The capacity of the minimal cut, and (by
Theorem

34
) the value of the maximum admissible flow, is given by:

F_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{1}(B)]

Now, consider the set
A^{\prime}=\{(s,x^{\prime})\in E\mid(s,x^{\prime})\notin A\}
. Since elements of
A^{\prime}
are not included in the cut, all vertices in
\pi_{2}(A^{\prime})
appear in
S
.
Because of that, all elements related by
R
with those elements must also be included in
S
, to avoid including the central edges of infinite capacity in the minimal cut. Hence
R(\pi_{2}(A^{\prime}))\subseteq S
, which implies that
R(\pi_{2}(A^{\prime}))\subseteq\pi_{1}(B)
.

Using these observations, we can bound the value of the maximal flow from below:

\displaystyle F_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}

\displaystyle=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{1}(B)]

\displaystyle\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(\pi_{2}(A^{\prime}))]

\displaystyle\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A^{\prime})]

(2)

\displaystyle=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times(X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})]

(
A
and
A^{\prime}
are disjoint)

Because of the admissibility constraint,
F_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
is also bounded from above by

\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}}c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times(X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})]

which means the lower bound given above is tight.
By symmetric reasoning, we can derive

F_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times(Y\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})]

In particular, this means that the flow at every edge connected to the source
s
must be at capacity, because the sum of
c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})
for
x^{\prime}\in V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}
is at its maximum permitted value.

Now, we define
\rho:R\to GR
in the following way.

1.

For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
, set
\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(o)
.

2.

For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At},\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R
, set
\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=f_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

Condition (1) of the coalgebraic definition of the bisimulation holds immediately. To see (2), observe that for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{At},p\in\mathsf{Act},x^{\prime}\in X
, we have that

\displaystyle\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in E_{\alpha,p}}f_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R}\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))

Condition (3) holds by symmetric argument.

For the converse, assume that conditions (2) and (3) of the coalgebraic definition hold. We show that (2) holds by contradiction.
Thus, assume the negation of condition (2) in the lemma statement, namely that for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act},A\subseteq X
we have that

\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]>\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

As before, the capacity of the minimal
(s,t)
-cut for each flow network
(G_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}},c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})
is given by

\displaystyle F_{\alpha,p}

\displaystyle=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{1}(B)]

\displaystyle\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(\pi_{1}(A^{\prime}))]

\displaystyle>\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\pi_{2}(A)]+\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(\pi_{1}(A^{\prime}))]

\displaystyle=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times(X\cap V_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}})]

Hence, the maximal flow is below capacity.
Thus there exists
x^{\prime}\in X
for which

\displaystyle\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle>f_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(s,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in E_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}}f_{\alpha,\mathsf{p}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in R}\rho(x,y)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))

which leads to contradiction.
Condition (3) holds by symmetry.
∎

See
8

Proof.

Assume
R
is a bisimulation.
Condition (1) holds immediately as a corollary of
Lemma

35
.
To show (2), take any equivalence class
Q\in{X}/{R}
.
Since by assumption
R
is an equivalence relation, observe that
R(Q)=Q=R^{-1}(Q)
.
Assume
(x,y)\in R
.
We can use
Lemma

35
to get the following for all
\alpha\in At
, and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}

\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]\leq\gamma(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]\leq\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

from which we can conclude (2).

For the converse, observe that condition (1) of
Lemma

35
holds immediately.
To see (2) take an arbitrary
A\subseteq X
.
Let
A/{R}
be the quotient of
A
by the relation
R
and let
X/{R}
be the quotient of
X
by
R
.
Observe that
A/{R}
is a partition of
A
and
X/{R}
is a partition of
X
.

Moreover, for each equivalence class
P\in A/{R}
, there exists an equivalence class
Q_{P}\in X/{R}
, such that
P\subseteq Q_{P}=R(P)
.
Because of monotonicity, we also have that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[{\mathsf{p}}\times P]\leq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{P}]
for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
.
By
\sigma
-additivity we have that

\displaystyle\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

\displaystyle=\beta(x)_{\alpha}\left[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\bigcup_{P\in A/{R}}P\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in A/{R}}\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times P]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{P\in A/{R}}\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{P}]

\displaystyle=\beta(y)_{\alpha}\left[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\bigcup_{P\in A/{R}}Q_{P}\right]

\displaystyle=\beta(y)_{\alpha}\left[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times\bigcup_{P\in A/{R}}R(P)\right]

\displaystyle=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

which proves (2) for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At},\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
. The case for (3) proceeds symmetrically.
∎

C.1
Order theoretic characterisation of bisimulations

The bisimulation functional
\Phi
(
Definition

9
) can be shown to be monotone, while bisimulations can be characterised as postfixed points of that operator.

Lemma 36
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras.
The following hold:

1.

\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}
is monotone with respect to inclusion order

2.

R
is a bisimulation between
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
if and only if
R\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(R)

Proof.

For (1), let
R\subseteq S\subseteq X\times Y
and assume that
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(R)
.
Then for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
we have that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(o)
.
For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
A\subseteq X
we have that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]\leq\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]
.
Since
R\subseteq S
, we have that
R(A)\subseteq S(A)
.
By monotonicity, it holds that
\gamma(y)_{\alpha}(\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A))\leq\gamma(y)[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times S(A)]
and therefore
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]\leq\gamma(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times S(A)]
.
The remaining case is symmetric.
Therefore
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(S)
, which proves that
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(R)\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(S)
.

(2) is trivial as it is rephrasing of
Lemma

35
.
∎

Recall that the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem states that the greatest fixpoint of a monotone endofunction
f:X\to X
on a complete lattice
(X,\sqsubseteq)
is given by the following

\mathsf{gfp}(f)=\bigsqcup\{x\in X\mid x\sqsubseteq f(x)\}

In the following, let
\sim_{\beta,\gamma}
denote the greatest bisimulation between
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
. The subscripts can be omitted when the coalgebras are obvious from the context.

Corollary 37
.

Let
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras. The greatest fixpoint
\mathsf{gfp}(\Phi_{\beta,\gamma})
of the functional
\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}
is the greatest bisimulation between
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
.

Proof.

Because of Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem,
\mathsf{gfp}(\Phi_{\beta,\gamma})
is a fixpoint, and in particular a post-fixed point.
Thus, by
Lemma

36
it is a bisimulation.

Let
S
be an arbitrary bisimulation between
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebras
(X,\beta)
and
(Y,\gamma)
.
Again, by
Lemma

36
we have that
S\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(S)
and therefore

S\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(S)\subseteq\bigcup\{R\subseteq X\times Y\mid R\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,\gamma}(R)\}=\mathsf{gfp}(\Phi_{\beta,\gamma})\qed

We can simplify the characterisation of
\Phi
when dealing with equivalence relations (similarly to
Lemma

8
).
In terms of notation, we will use
\Phi_{\beta}
to denote
\Phi_{\beta,\beta}
.

Lemma 38
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra and let
R\subseteq X\times X
be an equivalence relation.
We have
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
if and only if for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, the following are true.

1.

For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(y)_{\alpha}(o)

2.

For all
Q\in X/{R}
and for all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
, we have
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{p\}\times Q]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{p\}\times Q]
.

Moreover,
\Phi_{\beta}(R)
is also an equivalence relation.

Proof.

The proof of the first claim is identical to the proof of
Lemma

8
and hence we only focus on showing that that
\Phi_{\beta}(R)
is also an equivalence relation.

As for the latter claim, the first half of the lemma tells us that we can use the simpler characterisation of
\Phi_{\beta}
.
Reflexivity holds immediately, as for all
x\in X
and all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}

1.

For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)
.

2.

For all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
Q\in X/R
, we have
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]
.

and hence
(x,x)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
.

To see symmetry, assume that
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
for some
x,y\in X
. Then, because of symmetry of equality we have that all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}

1.

For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(y)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)
.

2.

For all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
Q\in X/R
, we have
\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]
.

and hence
(y,x)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
.

Finally, to see transitivity, assume that
(x,y),(y,z)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
for some arbitrary
x,y,z\in X
. Then again, by transitivity of equality we have that for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}

1.

For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
,
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(y)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(z)_{\alpha}(o)
.

2.

For all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
Q\in X/R
, we have
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\beta(z)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

and hence
(y,z)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)
.
∎

Since
\Phi_{\beta}
preserves equivalence relations, it can also be viewed as an endofunction on the lattice of equivalence relations, ordered by inclusion (refinement).
Because
\mathcal{G}
preserves weak pullbacks, the greatest bisimulation is an equivalence relation
[
57
, Corollary 5.6]
.
Thus, to characterise the greatest bisimulation, we can restrict ourselves to equivalence relations.

Before continuing, we recall some definitions.
An
\omega
-cochain
is a sequence
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
of elements of
X
, such that for all
i\in\omega
, we have that
x_{i}\sqsupseteq x_{i+1}
.
We call an endofunction
f:X\to X

\omega
-cocontinuous
if it preserves meets of
\omega
-cochains, that is
f\left(\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\right)=\sqcap_{i\in\omega}f(x_{i})
.

Proposition 39
.

Let
(X,\sqsubseteq)
be a complete lattice and
f:X\to X
an endofunction.

1.

(Kleene fixpoint theorem) If
f
is
\omega
-cocontinuous, then it possesses a greatest fixed point given by
\mathsf{gfp}(f)=\sqcap_{i\in\omega}f^{(i)}(\top)
where
f^{(0)}=\mathtt{id}
and
f^{(n+1)}=f^{(n)}\circ f
.

2.

If
f
is monotone then for every
\omega
-cochain
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
it holds that
f\left(\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\right)\sqsubseteq\sqcap_{i\in\omega}f(x_{i})
.

Proof.

We prove the second claim.
Take a descending
\omega
-cochain
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
.
Because of the monotonicity, applying
f
to the elements of that cochain yields another descending
\omega
-cochain
\{f(x_{i})\}_{i\in\omega}
.
Because
\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\sqsubseteq x_{i}
for all
i\in\omega
, by monotonicity we have that
f\left(\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\right)\sqsubseteq f(x_{i})
, which makes
f\left(\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\right)
a lower bound of the cochain
\{f(x_{i})\}_{i\in\omega}
and hence it is below its meet.
Therefore, it holds that
f\left(\sqcap_{i\in\omega}x_{i}\right)\sqsubseteq\sqcap_{i\in\omega}f(x_{i})
.
∎

Given a
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra
(X,\beta)
, we show the
\omega
-cocontinuity of
\Phi_{\beta}
on the lattice of equivalence relations on the set
X
, by readapting the result of Baier
[
5
, Lemma 3.7.5]
.

Lemma 40
.

Let
(X,\beta)
be
\mathcal{G}
-coalgebra.
Then
\Phi_{\beta}
is
\omega
-cocontinous on the lattice of equivalence relations on
X
.

Proof.

Because of
Proposition

39
and
Lemma

36
, to establish
\omega
-cocontinuity it suffices to show that for any descending
\omega
-cochain of equivalence relations
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
ordered by inclusion,

\bigcap_{i\in\omega}\Phi_{\beta}(R_{i})\subseteq\Phi_{\beta,}\left(\bigcap_{i\in\omega}R_{i}\right)

To this end, first note that for any
Q\in X/R
and
i\in\omega
there exists a unique
Q_{i}\in X/R_{i}
such that
Q\subseteq Q_{i}
, and so
Q\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in\omega}Q_{i}
.
Conversely, if
x\in Q_{i}
for all
i\in\omega
, then take any
y\in Q
(guaranteed to exist, since
Q
is an equivalence class).
Since
y\in Q_{i}
for all
i
, we have that
x\mathrel{R_{i}}y
for all
i\in\omega
, and so
x\mathrel{R}y
, meaning
x\in Q
.
This tells us that
\bigcap_{i\in\omega}Q_{i}\subseteq Q
, and hence
Q=\bigcap_{i\in\omega}Q_{i}
.
Furthermore, it follows that for
i\in\omega
we have
Q_{i+1}\subseteq Q_{i}
.

Now, fix
x\in X
; by monotonicity of probability, we find that

\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{0}]\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{1}]\geq\cdots\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

However, since
\beta(x)_{\alpha}
has finite support, this sequence can take only finitely many different values.
This means that it stabilises for some
i(x,Q)\in\omega
, meaning that for
k\geq i(x,Q)
it holds that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{i}]=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{i+1}]\geq\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]
.

Suppose now that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]<\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{i}]
for all
i
.
Then in particular there exists some
x^{\prime}\in X
with
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},x^{\prime})>0
, such that
x^{\prime}\in Q_{i}
for all
i\in\omega
, but
x^{\prime}\not\in Q
.
This would contradict that
Q=\bigcap_{i\in\omega}Q_{i}
, and so we conclude that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{i}]=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]
for some
i\in\omega
— in particular, this should hold for all
i\geq i(x,Q)
.

Now, given
(x,y)\in\bigcap_{i\in\omega}\Phi_{\beta}(R_{i})
, by
Lemma

38
we have that
\beta(x)_{\alpha}(o)=\beta(y)_{\alpha}(o)
for all
o\in 2+\mathsf{Out}
, and for all
i\in\omega
and
S\in X/R_{i}
we have
\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times S]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times S]
.
Given
Q\in X/R
, if we choose
k=\max(i(x,Q),i(y,Q))
, then the above tells us that

\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\beta(x)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{k}]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q_{k}]=\beta(y)_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

Both conditions of
Lemma

38
are now satisfied, and so
(x,y)\in\Phi_{\beta}(R)=\Phi_{\beta}(\bigcap_{i\in\omega}R_{i})
.
∎

See
10

Proof.

Follows from the
\omega
-cocontinuity of
\Phi_{\beta}
and
Proposition

39

∎

Let
A\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}
and
\mathsf{f},\in\mathtt{Exp}
.
We define
A/\mathsf{f}=A/\mathsf{f}=\{\mathsf{g}\in\mathtt{Exp}\mid\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}\in A\}
.

Lemma 41
.

If
R\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
is a congruence relation with respect to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
operators and
(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f})\in R
then
R(A/{\mathsf{e}})\subseteq R(A)/\mathsf{f}
.

Proof.

If
\mathsf{g}\in R(A/\mathsf{e})
, then there exists some
\mathsf{h}\in A/\mathsf{e}
such that
(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{g})\in R
.
Since
\mathsf{h}\in A/\mathsf{e}
, also
\mathsf{h}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{e}\in A
.
Because
R
is a congruence relation, we have that
(\mathsf{h}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{e},\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})\in R
and hence
\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}\in R(A)
, which in turn implies that
\mathsf{g}\in A/\mathsf{f}
.
∎

Lemma 42
.

For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\in\mathtt{Exp}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
A\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}
we have that

\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A/\mathsf{f}]+\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

Proof.

We derive as follows.

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{a}\in A}\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{a_{0}}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f}\in A}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a_{0}})+\sum_{\mathsf{a}\in A}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A/\mathsf{f}]+\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]\qed

When considering the Brzozowski coalgebra
(\mathtt{Exp},\partial)
, the operator
\Phi_{\partial}
has an another desirable property in addition to preserving equivalences.

Lemma 43
.

Let
R
be a congruence relation with respect to
\mathsf{ProbGKAT}
operators. Then
\Phi_{\partial}(R)
is also a congruence relation.

Proof.

Let
R\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}\times\mathtt{Exp}
be a congruence relation, and let
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h}\in\mathtt{Exp}
, such that
(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{g}),(\mathsf{f},\mathsf{h})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\in\mathtt{BExp}
and
{{r}}\in[0,1]
.
To show that
(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{h})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
, observe that for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
we have that

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle={{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(o)+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle={{r}}\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(o)+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(o)

Similarly, for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
Q\in\mathtt{Exp}/R
we have that

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{q}\in Q}\partial(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{q}\in Q}{{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})

\displaystyle={{r}}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

\displaystyle={{r}}\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]+{{(1-r)}}\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

and therefore
(\mathsf{e}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\oplus_{{r}}\mathsf{h})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)

To show that
(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{h})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
, consider the case when
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
.
For all
o\in\mathsf{2}+\mathsf{Out}
we then have that

\partial(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(o)=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(o)=\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(o)=\partial(\mathsf{g}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(o)

Similarly, for all
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
Q\in\mathtt{Exp}/R
we have that

\partial(\mathsf{e}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]=\partial(\mathsf{g}+_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times Q]

The case when
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}
follows analogously.

Now, we wish to show that
(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{h})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
. Instead of using the simpler characterisation of
\Phi
when dealing with equivalence relations from
Lemma

38
, we will establish the conditions of
Definition

9
. For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
, we have that

\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)=\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)=\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)

For all
\alpha\in\mathsf{At}
and
o\in\{\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.33 2.88 3.81 4.36 6.69 6.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}+\mathsf{Out}
it holds that

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(o)+\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(o)+\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(o)

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}(o)

Now, we use
Lemma

41
and
Lemma

42
to show that for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{Act}
,
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
A\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A/\mathsf{f}]+\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{f})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

\displaystyle\leq\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A/\mathsf{f})]+\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

\displaystyle\leq\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)/\mathsf{g}]+\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)\partial(\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

The proof of
\partial(\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times B]\leq\partial(\mathsf{g}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{h})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R^{-1}(B)]
for arbitrary
B\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}
is analogous.

Now, consider the case of showing that
(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})},\mathsf{f}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
. First assume that
\alpha\in\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}}
.
In such a case, we have that:

\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}=\partial(\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)

Since all probability mass is assigned to the element
\checkmark
, all remaining conditions of
\Phi_{\partial}
are immediately satisfied.
Now, assume that
\alpha\leq_{\mathsf{BA}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}}
.
First, consider the subcase when
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
. Since
(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{g})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
, it means that also
\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
.
Hence,

\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}(\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

Therefore, in such a subcase we have that
(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})},\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
.
For the remainder of this case, we can safely assume that
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)<{{1}}
and
\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)<1
. We have that

For all

it holds that

Again, we use
Lemma

41
to show that for arbitrary
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
A\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{a}\in A}\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{a_{o}}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\in A}\frac{\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a_{0}})}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}

\displaystyle=\frac{{{1}}}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times A/{\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{{1}}}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A/{\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{{1}}}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)/{\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{a_{o}}\mathbin{;}\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})}\in R(A)}\frac{\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{a_{0}})}{{{1}}-\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)}

\displaystyle=\partial(\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R(A)]

The proof that
\partial(\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times B]\leq\partial(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})_{\alpha}[\{\mathsf{p}\}\times R^{-1}(B)]
for arbitrary
B\subseteq\mathtt{Exp}
and
\mathsf{p}\in\mathsf{Act}
is symmetric.
We conclude that
(\mathsf{e}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})},\mathsf{g}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathsf{b}})})\in\Phi_{\partial}(R)
.

and
\partial(\mathsf{e})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
.

\partial(\mathsf{g})_{\alpha}(\checkmark)={{1}}
. Therefore, we have

\partial(\mathsf{e}^{[{{r}}]})_{\alpha}(\hbox to5.54pt{\vbox to5.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.34999pt\lower-0.34999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

which is enough to show that in that subcase indeed

.

, we have that
